Homogenisation of vectorial free-discontinuity functionals with cohesive type surface terms Thanks: Preprint SISSA 18/2024/MATE
Gianni Dal Maso, Davide Donati

Abstract
. The results on Γ \Gamma -limits of sequences of free-discontinuity functionals with bounded cohesive surface terms are extended to the case of vector-valued functions. In this framework, we prove an integral representation result for the Γ \Gamma -limit, which is then used to study deterministic and stochastic homogenisation problems for this type of functionals.



MSC codes:
49J45, 49Q20, 60G60, 74Q05, 74S60.

Keywords:

\Gamma
-convergence, Free-discontinuity Problems, Homogenisation, Stochastic Homogenisation, Integral Representation, Functionals Depending on Vector-valued Functions

1.
Introduction

Free-discontinuity problems are minimisation problems for functionals of the form

(1.1)

\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{J_{u}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

where

•

A
is a bounded open set in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,

•

f
and
g
are two given scalar functions,

•

the unknown
u
is a function defined in
A
with values in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
,

•

J_{u}
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional essential discontinuity set of
u
, whose location and size are unknown,

•

\nabla u
is the gradient of
u
in
A\setminus J_{u}
,

•

\nu_{u}
is the measure theoretical unit normal to
J_{u}
,

•

[u]=u^{+}-u^{-}
, where
u^{+}
and
u^{-}
are the traces of
u
on both sides of
J_{u}
,

•

{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

Since the function
u
exhibits an essential discontinuity set, a suitable framework to study these problems is the space
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
of functions of bounded variation. Problems of this type in
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
have been extensively studied (see for instance
[
7
, Chapter 4.6]
and
[
11
]
).

In his pioneering work
[
34
]
, Griffith introduced the idea that the crack growth in an elastic material is determined by the competition between the stored elastic energy and the energy spent to open a new portion of the crack. Adopting this point of view, Francfort and Marigo proposed in
[
29
]
a variational model to study crack growth which includes the requirement that at each time
t>0
the displacement
u(t)
of the elastic body minimises a free-discontinuity functional of the form (
1.1
), where
g
also depends on the cracks present before
t
. In these models, the crack at time
t
is the union of
J_{u(s)}
for
s\leq t
, the volume integral represents the stored elastic energy, while the surface integral is related the energy spent to produce the crack. For an overview on this subject we refer to
[
10
]
.

In cohesive models of fracture mechanics, it is natural to assume that the function
g
satisfies the following growth conditions

(1.2)

c(|\zeta|\land 1)\leq g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C(|\zeta|\land 1),

for suitable constants
0<c\leq C
, where for
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
s\land t=\min\{s,t\}
. In particular, the Dugdale model
[
26
]
can be reformulated in the language of
[
10
]
, using
g(\zeta):=a(|\zeta|\land b)
, for suitable constants
a,b\in[0,+\infty)
. Unfortunately, under hypotheses (
1.2
) the functional (
1.1
) is never lower semicontinuous in
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
with respect to the weak
∗
convergence. Under suitable hypotheses (see for instance
[
8
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
9
]
), its lower semicontinuous envelope has the form

(1.3)

\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

for different functions
f
and
g
, where
f
satisfy the growth conditions

(1.4)

c(|\xi|-1)\leq f(x,\xi)\leq C(|\xi|+1)\quad\text{for every $x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}$ and $\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}$},

and
f^{\infty}
is its recession function with respect
\xi
. Here and in the rest of the paper
D^{c}u
is the Cantor part of the measure
Du
(see
[
7
, Definition 3.91]
), and
dD^{c}u/d|D^{c}u|
is the Radon-Nikodým derivative of
D^{c}u
with respect to its total variation.

Since condition (
1.2
) does not allow us to control the total variation of
|Du|(A)
along a minimising sequence, to gain coerciveness it is convenient to extend the functional to a space larger than
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, where all terms of (
1.3
) can still be defined. We choose to extend these functionals to the space
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, studied by the second author in
[
24
]
, and defined using the scalar version
GBV_{\star}(A)
introduced in
[
19
]
. This is a vector space and, although
Du
is not defined for every
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, one can always define
\nabla u
and
D^{c}u
in a convenient way. Moreover, the bounds for a minimising sequence obtained from (
1.2
) are enough to apply a suitable compactness result with respect to convergence
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-a.e. (see
[
19
, Theorem 5.4]
,
[
21
, Theorem 7.13]
, and
[
24
, Theorem 4.8]
).

In this paper, we study the
\Gamma
-convergence with respect to the convergence in measure of sequences of functionals of the form (
1.3
), where
f
and
g
satisfy (
1.4
) and (
1.2
).
We first prove a compactness result (Theorem
4.1
), which shows that under suitable hypotheses (see Definitions
3.1
and
3.3
) on the integrands
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
corresponding to a sequence of functionals
(E_{n})_{n}
, there always exists a subsequence
\Gamma
-converging to a functional
E
, whose volume and jump terms can be written as integral functionals associated to some functions
f
and
g
(see Theorem
5.16
).

To obtain a complete integral representation of
E
as in (
1.3
) it remains to deal with the term depending on the Cantor part. This requires stronger hypotheses on
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
, studied in Section
6
, concerning quantitative estimates on the behaviour of
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
near
\infty
and near
0
, respectively. Under these assumptions, we show that
f
and
g
can be obtained by taking suitable limits of the infima of some minimisation problems for
E_{n}(\cdot,Q)
on suitable small cubes
Q
and that, if
f
is independent of
x
, we have the integral representation

E(u,A)=\int_{A}f(\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}.

These results are then applied to the case of homogenisation, where the functionals
E_{n}
are given by

(1.5)

E_{n}(u,A)=\int_{A}f\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{n}},\nabla u\Big)\,dx+\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{n}},\frac{D^{c}u}{|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}g\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{n}},[u],\nu_{u}\Big)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

for a sequence
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0^{+}
. We determine general conditions on
f
and
g
which imply that the
\Gamma
-limit of these sequence of functionals exists and has the form

(1.6)

E_{\rm hom}(u,A)=\int_{A}f_{\rm hom}(\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{A}f_{\rm hom}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}g_{\rm hom}([u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

for some functions
f_{\rm hom}
and
g_{\rm hom}
independent of
x
. These functions
f_{\rm hom}
and
g_{\rm hom}
are obtained by taking the limits of infima of certain minimisation problems for
E_{n}
, with
\varepsilon_{n}=1
, on cubes whose sides tend to infinity.

Thanks to these properties, we can apply the previous results to stochastic homogenisation problems, where
f
and
g
are random integrands satisfying suitable properties (see Definition
9.3
). Under these assumptions, we show that the sequence
E_{n}

\Gamma
-converges to
E
given by (
1.6
) almost surely.

These results were obtained in the scalar case in
[
21
,
20
]
.
In these papers, the truncation
u^{(m)}:=(u\land m)\lor(-m)
and the corresponding estimates for the functionals are frequently used. The main difficulty in the vector-valued setting is that these truncations have to be replaced by a sort of
smooth
truncations of the form
\psi_{m}^{i}\circ u
,
i\in\{1,...,m\}
for suitable functions
\psi_{m}^{i}\in C^{\infty}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{k};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
satisfying

\psi_{m}^{i}(y)=y\quad\text{for $|y|\leq R_{m}$ \quad and \quad}\psi_{m}^{i}(y)=0\quad\text{ for $|y|\geq\sigma^{m}R_{m}$},

where the sequence
R_{m}\to+\infty
and the constant
\sigma>2
are prescribed. These types of truncations were already considered in previous works (see, for instance,
[
12
,
13
,
16
,
28
,
31
]
). The main difficulty here is that an estimate for
E_{n}({\psi_{m}^{i}\circ u},A)
in terms of
E_{n}(u,A)
cannot be obtained for every
i\in\{1,...,m\}
, but only for a suitable choice of
i
, depending on
n,m,u,
and
A
. This requires deep changes in many technical results of
[
21
]
and
[
20
]
, which introduce new terms depending on
m
and which cannot be neglected (see, for instance, Lemma
5.9
and Proposition
6.14
).

Our results can be considered as a preliminary step for the study of the asymptotic behaviour of the crack growth in a heterogeneous cohesive material, when the size of the grains tends to zero. Indeed, according to the approach of
[
40
]
(see also
[
36
,
39
,
38
,
41
]
), in the variational theory of rate-independent problems the convergence of the quasi-static evolutions requires the
\Gamma
-convergence of the corresponding energy-dissipation functionals and the existence of a joint recovery sequence. Our results give a complete answer to the first part of this program in the case of cohesive models for fracture. In this paper we do not address the construction of a joint recovery sequence.

The problem of homogenisation of free-discontinuity functionals has already been addressed in a wide variety of cases. In the now classical work
[
12
]
, Braides, Defranceschi, and Vitali first dealt with the deterministic periodic case for integrands
f
and
g
satisfying

(1.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle c|\xi|^{p}\leq f(x,\xi)\leq C(1+|\xi|^{p})\quad\text{for every $x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}$ and $\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}$},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle c(1+|\zeta|)\leq g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C(1+|\zeta|)\quad\text{for every }x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d},\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k},\nu\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}

for some suitable constants
c,C>0
and for
p>1
. Under these growth conditions one can use the compactness results on
SBV
by Ambrosio
[
5
, Theorem 2.1]
(see also
[
4
,
6
]
) and the functionals to homogenise are of type (
1.1
). The main result of their paper is a periodic homogenisation theorem for such functionals. They also show that, in this case, the bulk integrand
f_{\rm hom}
of the
\Gamma
-limit is determined only by
f
and the surface integrand
g_{\rm hom}
is determined only by
g
.

Later Giacomini and Ponsiglione studied the scalar case
k=1
in
[
32
]
, assuming that
f
satisfies (
1.7
) and that
g
does not depend on
\zeta
and

c\leq g(x,\nu)\leq C\quad\text{ for every }x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\text{ and }\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

Note that in their result no periodicity assumption on
f
and
g
is made. As in the periodic case studied by Braides et al.
[
12
]
, under these hypotheses they are able to show that
f_{\rm hom}
dependes only on
f
and that
g_{\rm hom}
depends only on
g
.

More recently, Cagnetti et al. considered the vectorial case
k\geq 1
in
[
13
]
with non-periodic integrands
f
and
g
, where
f
satisfies (
1.7
) and
g
satisfies

(1.9)

c\leq g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq C(1+|\zeta|)\quad\text{for every $x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}$, $\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}$, and $\nu\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}$}.

In a subsequent work
[
14
]
, the results of
[
13
]
are then employed to deal with the stochastic homogenisation of free-discontinuity integral functionals satisfying growth conditions (
1.7
) and (
1.9
). Under the standard assumptions of stochastic homogenisation they prove an almost sure
\Gamma
-convergence result for functionals of type (
1.1
).

Building on the techniques devised in
[
13
,
14
]
, the same authors tackled in
[
15
]
the problem of deterministic and stochastic homogenisation for sequences of type (
1.5
) under the hypotheses that both
f
and
g
have linear growth. In this case, the underlying function space becomes
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and the integral depending on the Cantor part of the derivative has to be considered in (
1.5
).

Recently, the problem of homogenisation of free-discontinuity functionals was also addressed in the context of functions of bounded deformation.
In
[
30
]
, Friedrich, Peruguini, and Solombrino tackle in dimension
d=2
the
\Gamma
-convergence with respect to the convergence in measure of functionals

E_{n}
given by

(1.10)

E_{n}(u,A)=\int_{A}f\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{n}},\mathcal{E}u\Big)\,dx+\int_{A}g\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon_{n}},[u],\nu_{u}\Big)\,{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

where
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is open and bounded,
u\in GSBD^{p}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and

\mathcal{E}u=(\nabla u+\nabla u^{T})/2
is the approximate symmetric gradient of
u
, assuming that the integrand
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}_{\rm sym}\to[0,+\infty)
has
p
-growth and that
g
is bounded from below by a positive constant.

In a recent work
[
25
]
, the stochastic homogenisation of functionals of type (
1.10
) restricted to piecewise rigid functions, i.e., functions
u(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(R_{i}x+b_{i})\chi_{E_{i}}
for
R_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
skew-symmetric,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
and
E_{i}
of finite perimeter, was tackled in dimensions
d\geq 2
.

Our paper is organised as follows. In Section
2
we fix the notation and lay out the basic tools used throughout the paper.
We then introduce in Section
3
the collections of volume integrands
\mathcal{F}
and surface integrands
\mathcal{G}
which will be the object of our study; we also introduce a class of abstract functionals
\mathfrak{E}
which contains the integral functionals corresponding to integrands in
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Section
4
is devoted to proving a compactness result for sequences of integral functionals. In Section
5
we investigate the properties of the class
\mathfrak{E}
, by proving an integral representation result for
\mathfrak{E}
, showing that if
E\in\mathfrak{E}
is lower-semicontinuous with respect to the convergence in measure then its “absolutely continuous part”
E^{a}
and its “jump part”
E^{j}
can be represented by integral functionals, with integrands
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
, respectively.

In Section
6
we introduce two smaller collections of integrands
\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\vartheta}
and study the
\Gamma
-limits of sequences of integral functionals associated with them. We then prove in Section
7
that under some suitable hypotheses these
\Gamma
-limits can be fully represented as the sum of three integral functionals as in (
1.3
), including the term depending on the Cantor part.

In Section
8

we exploit the representation result of Section
7
to give a necessary sufficient condition for the
\Gamma
-convergence of sequences of functionals of the form (
1.3
) with
f\in\mathcal{F}^{\alpha}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}^{\vartheta}
. Finally, Section
9
is devoted to the study of the
\Gamma
-limit of functionals of type (
1.5
). As an application, these results are then employed in the final part of Section
9
to deal with stochastic homogenisation.

2.
Notation and preliminaries

In this Section we fix the notation and introduce the basic tools used in the rest of the paper.

(a)

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. The scalar product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
\cdot
and the Euclidean norm of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
|\,\,|
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the
i
-th component of
x
is denoted by
x_{i}
. For every
\rho>0
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
the open ball of radius
\rho
and center
x
is denoted by
B_{\rho}(x)
.

(b)

We fix once and for all two positive integer numbers
d\geq 1
,
k\geq 1
. the unit spere in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
is denote by
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:=\{\nu\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}:|\nu|=1\}
, . We also set
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\pm}:=\{\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:\pm\nu_{i(\nu)}>0\}
, where
i(\nu)\in\{1,...,d\}
is the largest index such that
\nu^{i(\nu)}\neq 0
.

(c)

Vectors in
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
are identified with
1\times d
matrices, while
{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
is identified with the space of all
k\times d
matrices.
For
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
and
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

\xi x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
is defined by the usual rules of matrix multiplication and
\xi^{i}
is the
i
-th row of
\xi
.
Given a matrix
\xi=(\xi_{ij})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
, its Frobenius norm is defined by

|\xi|:=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\xi_{ij}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.

(d)

For
\rho>0
we set
Q(\rho):=\{y\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}:|y\cdot e_{i}|<\rho/2\}
, where
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
is the canonical basis of
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
. Given
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
, we set
Q(x,\rho):=x+Q(\rho)
.

(e)

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the space of all
n\times n
orthonormal matrices
R
with
\text{det}(R)=1
is denoted by
SO(n)
. For every
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we fix once and for all an element
R_{\nu}\in SO(d)
such that
R_{\nu}(e_{d})=\nu
. We suppose that
R_{e_{d}}=I
, the identity matrix, that the restrictions of
\nu\to\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
to
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\pm}
is continuous, and that
R_{\nu}(Q(\rho))=R_{-\nu}(Q(\rho))
for every
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
(see
[
14
, Example A.1]
for the proof of the existence of such
\nu\mapsto R_{\nu}
).

(f)

For
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\lambda\geq 1
, and
\rho>0
, we consider the rectangle

Q^{\lambda}_{\nu}(x,\rho):=x+R_{\nu}\Big(\Big(-\frac{\lambda\rho}{2},\frac{\lambda\rho}{2}\Big)^{d-1}\times\Big(-\frac{\rho}{2},\frac{\rho}{2}\Big)\Big);

we omit the indication of
\lambda
when
\lambda=1
.

(g)

Given an open set
\Omega\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
(resp.
\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
) is the collection of all open (resp. Borel) sets
A\subset\Omega
. Given
A,B\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
,
A\subset\subset B
means that
A
is relatively compact in
B
. We set
\mathcal{A}_{c}(\Omega):=\{A\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)\colon\,A\subset\subset\Omega\}
.

(h)

For every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
,
\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the two functions
\ell_{\xi}\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
and
u_{x,\zeta,\nu}\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
are defined for every
y\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\ell_{\xi}(y):=\xi y,

\displaystyle\displaystyle u_{x,\zeta,\nu}(y):=\begin{cases}\zeta\quad\text{ if }(y-x)\cdot\nu\geq 0,\\
0\quad\text{ if }(y-x)\cdot\nu<0.\end{cases}

Moreover, we set
\Pi^{\nu}_{x}:=\{x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\colon(y-x)\cdot\nu=0\}
.

(i)

Given
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, the space of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
-valued bounded Radon measures on
A
is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{b}(A;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
n=1
we omit the indication of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d})
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(A)
is non-negative,
d\mu/d\lambda
denotes the Radon-Nikodým derivative of
\mu
with respect to
\lambda
.
The Lebesgue measure is denoted by
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
and the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure is denoted by
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
.
For
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d})
the total variation
|\mu|
is computed with respect to the Frobenius norm.

(j)

For every
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
L^{0}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is the space of all
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-measurable functions
u\colon A\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
with the topology induced by the convergence in measure. We recall that such topology is metrisable and separable. When
n=1
the indication
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is omitted.

(k)

Given an
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-measurable set
E\subset\Omega
, a point
x\in\Omega
such that

\limsup_{\rho\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(E\cap B_{\rho}(x))}{\rho^{d}}>0,

and an
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-measurable function
u\colon E\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, we say that
a\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
is the approximate limit of
u
at
x
, in symbols

\underset{\underset{y\in E}{y\to x}}{\text{ap}\lim\,}u(y)=a,

if for every
\varepsilon>0
we have

\lim_{\rho\to 0^{+}}\frac{{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(\{|u-a|>\varepsilon\}\cap B_{\rho}(x))}{\rho^{d}}=0,

where
\{|u-a|>\varepsilon\}:=\{y\in E\colon|u(y)-a|>\varepsilon\}
.

(l)

Given
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and an
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-measurable function
u\colon A\to{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, the jump set
J_{u}
is the set of all points
x\in A
such that
there exists a triple
(u^{+}(x),u^{-}(x),\nu_{u}(x))\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, with
u^{+}(x)\neq u^{-}(x)
, such that, setting

H^{+}:=\{y\in A\colon\,(y-x)\cdot\nu_{u}(x)>0\}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,H^{-}:=\{y\in A\colon\,(y-x)\cdot\nu_{u}(x)<0\},

we have

\underset{\underset{y\in H^{+}}{y\to x}}{\text{ap}\lim\,}u(y)=u^{+}(x)\text{ \,\,\, and }\,\,\,\underset{\underset{y\in H^{-}}{y\to x}}{\text{ap}\lim\,}u(y)=u^{-}(x).

The triple
(u^{+}(x),u^{-}(x),\nu_{u}(x))
is well-defined up to interchanging the roles of
u^{+}(x)
and
u^{-}(x)
and swapping the sign of
\nu_{u}(x)
.

(m)

Given
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
the symbol
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
denotes the space of
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
-valued functions with bounded variation on
A
. We refer the reader to
[
7
]
(see also
[
11
,
27
,
33
]
) for an exhaustive introduction to this function space.
We recall that if
u\in BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
then for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e.
x\in A\setminus J_{u}
there exists

\tilde{u}(x):=\underset{\underset{y\in A}{y\to x}}{\text{ap}\lim\,}u(y).

We recall also that if
u\in BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, then
J_{u}
is a
(d-1)
-countably rectifiable set, and for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e.
x\in J_{u}
the vector
\nu_{u}(x)
is a measure theoretical normal to
J_{u}
. For every
x\in J_{u}
we set

[u](x):=u^{+}(x)-u^{-}(x).

A change of sign of
\nu_{u}(x)
obviously implies a change of sign in
[u](x)
.

(n)

Given
u\in BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, its distributional derivative
Du
, which is by definition a bounded
{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
-valued Radon measure, can be decomposed as

Du=\nabla u{\mathcal{L}}^{d}+D^{c}u+[u]\otimes\nu_{u}{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u},

where

•

\nabla u\in L^{1}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d})
is the approximate gradient of
u
, that is, the only
{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
-valued function such that for
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-a.e
x\in A
we have

(2.1)

\underset{\underset{y\in A}{y\to x}}{\text{ap}\lim\,}\frac{u(y)-\tilde{u}(x)-\nabla u(x)(y-x)}{|y-x|}=0,

•

D^{c}u
, called the Cantor part of
Du
, is a measure singular with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
and vanishing on all Borel sets
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
with
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(B)<+\infty
,

•

\otimes
denotes the tensor product defined by
(a\otimes b)_{ij}=a_{i}b_{j}
for
a\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
,
b\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u}
is the Borel measure on
A
defined by
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(B):={\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(B\cap J_{u})
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
, and
[u]\otimes\nu_{u}{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u}
denotes the measure with density
[u]\otimes\nu_{u}
with respect to
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u}
.

We briefly recall the definition and the main properties of the space
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, introduced in the scalar setting by the first author and Toader in
[
19
]
and in the vectorial setting by the second author in
[
24
]
. For
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we set
s\land t=\min\{s,t\}
and
s\lor t=\max\{s,t\}
. For every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
and
m>0
, we set
t^{(m)}:=(t\land m)\lor(-m)
and
a^{(m)}:=(a_{1}^{(m)},...,a_{k}^{(m)})
. In the rest of the section,
A\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
will always be a bounded open set.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
u\in L^{0}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
if and only
u^{(m)}\in BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
for every
m>0
and there exists
M>0
such that

(2.2)

\sup_{m>0}\int_{A}|\nabla u^{(m)}|\,dx+|D^{c}u^{(m)}|(A)+\int_{J_{u^{(m)}}}|[u^{(m)}]|\land 1\,d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq M.

GBV_{\star}(A)
is defined similarly for scalar functions.

Remark 2.2
.

It follows immediately from the definition that
u
belongs to
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
if and only if each component
u_{i}
belongs to
GBV_{\star}(A)
. By
[
21
, Theorem 3.9]
this implies that
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
is a vector space.

To characterise
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
by means of smooth truncations we introduce the following functions.

Given a positive constant
\sigma>2
, we fix a smooth radial function
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{k};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
satisfying

(2.3)

\begin{cases}\psi(y)=y&\text{ if }|y|\leq 1,\\
\psi(y)=0&\text{ if }|y|\geq\sigma,\\
|\psi(y)|\leq\sigma,\\
\textup{Lip}(\psi)=1.\end{cases}

It is not difficult to construct such a function (see for instance
[
13
, Section 4]
).

Given
R>0
, we set

(2.4)

\psi_{R}(y):=R\psi\Big(\frac{y}{R}\Big)\quad\text{ for every $y\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}$}.

Note that
\psi_{R}
satisfies

(2.5)

\begin{cases}\psi_{R}(y)=y&\text{ if }|y|\leq R,\\
\psi_{R}(y)=0&\text{ if }|y|\geq\sigma R,\\
|\psi_{R}(y)|\leq\sigma R,\\
\textup{Lip}(\psi_{R})=1.\end{cases}

The following proposition characterises
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
in terms of smooth truncations.

Proposition 2.3

(
[
24
, Proposition 3.8]
)
.

For every
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
there exists a constant
C_{u}>0
such that for every Lipschitz function
\phi
with compact support the function
v:=\phi\circ u
belongs to
BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and satisfies the inequality

(2.6)

\int_{A}|\nabla v|\,dx+|D^{c}v|(A)+\int_{J_{v}}|[v]|\land 1\,d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq C_{u}(\textup{Lip}(\phi)\lor 1).

Conversely, if
u\in L^{0}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and there exists a constant
C_{u}>0
such that for every integer
R>0
(
2.6
) holds with
v=\psi_{R}\circ u
and
\phi=\psi_{R}
then
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
.

In the following proposition we recall the fine properties of functions in
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then

(a)

for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e
x\in A\setminus J_{u}
there exists

\tilde{u}(x):=\underset{{y\to x}}{\textup{ap}\lim\,}u(y);

(b)

there exists a Borel function
\nabla u\in L^{1}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d})
such that for
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-a.e.
x\in A
formula (
2.1
) holds true; moreover, for every
R>0
we have

\nabla u(x)=\nabla(\psi_{R}\circ u)(x)\quad\text{for ${\mathcal{L}}^{d}$-a.e. }x\in\{|u|\leq R\};

(c)

there exists a unique Radon measure
D^{c}u\in\mathcal{M}_{b}(A;{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d})
such that for every
R>0
we have
D^{c}u(B)=0
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
with
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(B\setminus J_{u})=0
and
D^{c}u(B)=D^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)(B)
for every
B\subset\{x\in A\setminus J_{u}\colon\,\widetilde{u}(x)\text{ exists and }|\widetilde{u}(x)|\leq R\}
; moreover, for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle D^{c}u(B)=\lim_{R\to+\infty}D^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)(B),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle|D^{c}u|(B)=\lim_{R\to+\infty}|D^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)|(B);

(d)

for every
R>0
we have
J_{\psi_{R}\circ u}\subset J_{u}
up to an
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-negligible set and
|[\psi_{R}\circ u]|\leq|[u]|
on
J_{\psi_{R}\circ u}\cap J_{u}
. Moreover, for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e
x
in
J_{u}
and every
R>|u^{+}(x)|\lor|u^{-}(x)|
we have
|[\psi_{R}\circ u](x)|=|[u](x)|
.

For the proof of these facts we refer the reader to
[
24
, Proposition 3.7]
.

We conclude this section recalling some useful facts related to the Cantor part of compositions with smooth function with compact support.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
\phi\in C^{1}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{k};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then

(i)

\nabla(\phi\circ u)=\nabla\phi(\widetilde{u})\nabla u\quad\text{${\mathcal{L}}^{d}$-a.e. in $A$};

(ii)

D^{c}(\phi\circ u)=\nabla\phi(\widetilde{u})D^{c}u
as Radon measures on
A
;

(iii)

for every
R>0
we have

\frac{dD^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)}{d|D^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)|}=\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\quad\text{$|D^{c}u|$-a.e. in $\ A^{R}_{u,0}$},

where
A^{R}_{u,0}:=\{x\in A\colon\widetilde{u}(x)\text{ exists and }|\widetilde{u}(x)|\leq R\}
.
As a consequence we have

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\frac{dD^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)}{d|D^{c}(\psi_{R}\circ u)|}=\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\quad\text{ $|D^{c}u|$-a.e. in $A$}.

For the proof we refer the reader to
[
24
]
.
In accordance with the notation of
[
21
,
20
]
and
[
24
]
, we introduce two functionals on
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
closely related to the space
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
.

Definition 2.6
.

Given
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, with components
u_{1},...,u_{k}
, for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we define

V(u,A):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\int_{A}|\nabla u_{i}|\,dx+|D^{c}u_{i}|(A)+\int_{J_{u_{i}}\cap A}|[u_{i}]|\land 1\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\Big),\\

if
u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A)
and we set
V=+\infty
otherwise.
The definition is then extended to
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
by setting

V(u,A):=\sup\{V(u,A^{\prime}):\,\,A^{\prime}\subset A,\,\,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\}

and then to
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
by setting

V(u,B):=\inf\{V(u,A):\,\,B\subset A,\,\,A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\}.

Definition 2.7
.

Given
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we define

V_{2}(u,A):=\int_{A}|\nabla u|\,dx+|D^{c}u|(A)+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}|[u]|\land 1\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

if
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and we set
V_{2}(u,A)=+\infty
otherwise. The definition is then extended to every Borel set as in Definition
2.6
.

Remark 2.8
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and let
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. It follows immediately from Remark
2.2
that

u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\text{ \,\,if and only if\,\, }V(u,A)<+\infty.

Remark 2.9
.

It follows from
[
8
, Theorem 2.1]
and
[
19
, Theorem 3.11]
that the functional

V
of Definition
2.6
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the topology of
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. We don’t know whether this property holds for the functional
V_{2}
of Definition
2.7
.

3.
Volume and Surface integrands

Throughout the rest of the paper we fix six constants
c_{1},...,c_{6}\geq 0
and a bounded continuous function
\tau\colon[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
. We assume that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle 0<c_{1}\leq 1\leq c_{3}\leq c_{5},\,\,\,\,c_{6}\geq(c_{3}/c_{1})k^{3/2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau(0)=0\,\,\,\,\text{ and\,\,\,\, }\tau(t)\geq c_{3}(t\land 1)\,\,\,\text{ for every $t\geq 0$}.

We now introduce the collection of volume integrands considered in this paper.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the space of functions
f\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
satisfying the following conditions:

(f1)

f
is Borel measurable;

(f2)

c_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{i}|-c_{2}\leq f(x,\xi)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
;

(f3)

f(x,\xi)\leq c_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{i}|+c_{4}

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
;

(f4)

|f(x,\xi)-f(x,\xi)|\leq c_{5}|\xi-\eta|

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

and
\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
.

Remark 3.2
.

It follows from the inequalities
|\xi|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{i}|\leq k^{1/2}|\xi|
that

(f2
′
)

c_{1}|\xi|-c_{2}\leq f(x,\xi)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d},

(f3
′
)

f(x,\xi)\leq c_{3}k^{1/2}|\xi|+c_{4}

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}

and
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
.

The following definition introduces the collection of surface integrands considered in this paper.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the space of functions
g:{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
that satisfy the following conditions:

(g1)

g
is Borel measurable;

(g2)

c_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(|\zeta_{i}|\land 1)\leq g(x,\zeta,\nu)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
;

(g3)

g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq c_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(|\zeta_{i}|\land 1)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
;

(g4)

|g(x,\zeta,\nu)-g(x,\theta,\nu)|\leq\tau(|\zeta-\theta|)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\zeta,\theta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
;

(g5)

g(x,-\zeta,-\nu)=g(x,\zeta,\nu)

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
;

(g6)

for every
\zeta,\theta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
with
c_{6}k|\zeta|\leq|\theta|
we have
g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq g(x,\theta,\nu)
for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Remark 3.4
.

A variant of property (g6) was already used in
[
13
]
. Combining (g2) and (g3), it is easy to show that
g(x,\zeta,\nu)\leq c_{3}/c_{1}g(x,\theta,\nu)
whenever
\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\zeta_{i}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\theta_{i}|
. Arguing as in
[
13
, Remark 3.2]
we can show that this property and (g6) are weaker than monotonicity in
|\zeta|
of
g
.

Definition 3.5
.

For every
f\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
, the recession function
f^{\infty}\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
(with respect to
\xi
) is the function defined by

(3.2)

f^{\infty}(x,\xi):=\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\frac{f(x,t\xi)}{t}

for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
and for every
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
.

Remark 3.6
.

For every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
the function
\xi\mapsto f^{\infty}(x,\xi)
is positively
1
-homogeneous. If for every
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
the function
\xi\mapsto f(x,\xi)
is convex, the
\limsup
in (
3.2
) is a limit. If
f
satisfies (f2) and (f3), then
f^{\infty}
satisfies

(3.3)

c_{1}|\xi|\leq c_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{i}|\leq f^{\infty}(x,\xi)\leq c_{3}k^{1/2}|\xi|\quad\text{for every $x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}$ and $\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}$}.

If
f
satisfies (f4), then
f^{\infty}
satisfies

|f^{\infty}(x,\xi)-f^{\infty}(x,\eta)|\leq c_{5}|\xi-\eta|\quad\text{for every $x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}$ and $\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}$}.

In Section
8
we will consider also integrands
g^{0}
that do not belong to
\mathcal{G}
. For this reason, in the following definition we do not assume
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 3.7
.

Given
f\in\mathcal{F}
and a Borel function
g\colon{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to[0,+\infty)
satisfying (g5), the functional
E^{f,g}:L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\to[0,+\infty]
is the functional defined for
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})

by

E^{f,g}(u,A):=\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{D^{c}u}{|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap A}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

if
u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and by
E^{f,g}(u,A)=+\infty
otherwise.
The definition is then extended to
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
by setting

E^{f,g}(u,A):=\sup\{E^{f,g}(u,A^{\prime}):\,\,A^{\prime}\subset A,\,\,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\},

and then to
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
by setting

E^{f,g}(u,B):=\inf\{E^{f,g}(u,A):\,\,B\subset A,\,\,A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\}.

To study the integral representation of
\Gamma
-limits of sequences of functionals of the form
E^{f,g}
with
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
, it is convenient to study the properties of functionals
E^{f,g}
that pass to the
\Gamma
-limit. This leads to define the abstract space
\mathfrak{E}
of functionals defined on
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. We shall prove that the
\Gamma
-limit of a sequence of functionals in
\mathfrak{E}
belongs to
\mathfrak{E}
(see Theorem
4.1
) and that for every lower semicontinuous functional in
\mathfrak{E}
the volume and surface part admit and integral representation of the form

\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{J_{u}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

with
integrands
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
(see Theorem
5.16
).

For technical reasons, in the definition of the space
\mathfrak{E}
, we consider the behaviour of the functionals with respect to some smooth truncations. To this aim, for every
R>0
and every integer
i\geq 1
we consider
the function
\psi_{R}^{i}
defined by (
2.4
) with
R
replaced by
\sigma^{i-1}R
and
\sigma=c_{6}k+2
. Note that by (
2.5
) for every integer
i\geq 1
we have

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\psi^{i}_{R}(y)=y&\text{ if }|y|\leq\sigma^{i-1}R,\\
\psi^{i}_{R}(y)=0&\text{ if }|y|\geq\sigma^{i}R,\\
|\psi^{i}_{R}(y)|\leq\sigma^{i}R,\\
\textup{Lip}(\psi_{R})=1.\end{cases}

We are now in a position to introduce the space of abstract functionals
\mathfrak{E}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{E}
be the space of functionals
E\colon L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\to[0,+\infty]
satisfying the following conditions:

(a)

for every
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and for every
u,v\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
with
u=v

{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
-a.e. in
A
, we have
E(u,A)=E(v,A)
;

(b)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
the set function
B\mapsto E(u,B)
is a non-negative Borel measure on
{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
and for every
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

E(u,B)=\inf\{E(u,A)\colon A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\text{ and }B\subset A\};

(c1)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

c_{1}V(u,B)-c_{2}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B)\leq E(u,B);

(c2)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

E(u,B)\leq c_{3}V(u,B)+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B);

(d)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, and
a\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
we have

E(u+a,B)=E(u,B);

(e)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times d}
,
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

E(u+\ell_{\xi},B)\leq E(u,B)+c_{5}|\xi|{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B);

(f)

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
x\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}
,
\zeta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
, and
\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have

E(u+u_{x,\zeta,\nu},B)\leq E(u,B)+\tau(|\zeta|){\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(B\cap\Pi_{x}^{\nu});

(g)

for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and
R>0
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E(w+\psi^{i}_{R}\circ(u-w),B)

\displaystyle\leq E(u,B)+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{B^{R}_{u,w}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B^{R}_{u,w})

(3.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C}{m}\Big(E(u,B)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B)+\int_{B}|\nabla w|\,dx\Big),

where
B^{R}_{u,w}:=\{x\in B\colon|u(x)-w(x)|\geq R\}
and
C:=\max\{9c_{3}k/c_{1},2c_{3}k^{1/2},c_{4}\}
;

(h)

for every
0<\lambda\leq 1/(c_{6}k)
,
R\in SO(k)
,
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, and
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
we have

E(\lambda Ru,B)\leq E(u,B)+(c_{4}+c_{2}){\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B).

The subspace of all functionals
E\in\mathfrak{E}
such that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
the functional
E(\cdot,A)
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the topology of
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
is denoted by
\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
.

Remark 3.9
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{E}
,
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, and
u\in L^{0}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. We can define
E(u,B)
for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
by extending the function
u
to a function
v\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and setting
E(u,B):=E(v,B)
. The value of
E(u,B)
does not depend on the chosen extension thanks to the locality property (a) of Definition
3.8
.

Remark 3.10
.

The inequalities (c1) and (c2) imply that

(c1
′
)

c_{1}V_{2}(u,B)-c_{2}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(B)\leq E(u,B)

(c2
′
)

E(u,B)\leq c_{3}kV_{2}(u,B)+c_{4},

for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.
This follows from the elementary inequalities
|\xi|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\xi_{i}|\leq k^{1/2}|\xi|
and
|\zeta|\land 1\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}|[\zeta_{i}]|\land 1\leq k(|\zeta|\land 1)
, where
\xi_{i}
is the
i
-th row of the matrix
\xi
and
\zeta_{i}
is the
i
-th component of
\zeta
.

Remark 3.11
.

The technical condition (g) replaces the simpler condition (g) of
[
21
, Definition 3.1]
, which in the particular case
w_{1}=-m
and
w_{2}=m
reads as

(3.6)

E(u^{(m)},A)\leq E(u,A)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(\{|u|>m\}).

Although this truncation procedure is still available in the vector-valued case (see
[
24
, Definition 3.1, Proposition 3.8]
), acting componentwise, an estimate of the form (
3.6
)
does not hold for
k>1
, even for the prototypical integral functional
V_{2}
. This is one of the main difficulties in generalising these results to the vector-valued case. The new condition (g) of Definition
3.8
is crucial to introduce a different type of truncation operators with good estimates. Similar smooth truncations were already considered in
[
12
,
13
,
16
,
28
,
31
]
.

Remark 3.12
.

It follows directly from Remark
2.8
and from Definition
3.8
that if
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
then

E(u,A)<+\infty\,\,\,\text{ if and only if }\,\,\,u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}).

The following proposition shows that functionals of type
E^{f,g}
belong to
\mathfrak{E}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}
. Then the functional
E^{f,g}
belongs to
\mathfrak{E}
and for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. Moreover,
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
we have

(3.7)

E^{f,g}(u,B)=\int_{B}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{B}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{D^{c}u}{|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{J_{u}\cap B}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

for every
B\in\mathcal{B}(A)
.

Proof.

The proof of the fact that
E^{f,g}
satisfies (
3.7
) and (a)-(f) of Definition
3.8
may be deduced from the proof of
[
21
, Proposition 3.11]
, with minor changes.

We are left with showing that
E^{f,g}
enjoys properties (g) and (h) of Definition
3.8
. We begin proving (g). Since
E^{f,g}
satisfies properties (a) and (b), it is enough to prove (
3.5
) for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Let
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, let
R>0
, let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
i\in\{1,...,m\}
. We set
v^{i}_{R}:=w+\psi^{i}_{R}\circ(u-w)
and note that
v_{R}^{i}|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, thanks to Proposition
2.3
and to the fact that
GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
is a vector space.

Consider the set
A_{\rm reg}:=\{x\in A\colon\widetilde{u}(x),\,\widetilde{w}(x)\,\,\,\text{exist}\}
. We observe that by Proposition
2.4

A_{\rm reg}
and
J_{u}
are Borel sets and that
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}(A\setminus(A_{\rm reg}\cup J_{u}))=0
. We set

\displaystyle\displaystyle A_{\rm in}^{i}:=\{x\in A_{\rm reg}\colon|\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\leq\sigma^{i-1}R\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle A^{i}:=\{x\in A_{\rm reg}\colon\sigma^{i-1}R<|\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i}R\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle A^{i}_{\rm out}:=\{x\in A_{\rm reg}\colon|\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\geq\sigma^{i}R\}.

Thanks to (i) of Proposition
2.5
, for every
i\in\{1,...,m\}
we have that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nabla v^{i}_{R}=\nabla u\quad{\mathcal{L}}^{d}\textup{-}\text{a.e. in }A^{i}_{\rm in},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nabla v^{i}_{R}=\nabla w+\nabla\psi^{i}_{R}\circ(u-w)(\nabla u-\nabla w)\quad{\mathcal{L}}^{d}\textup{-}\text{a.e. in }A^{i},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\nabla v^{i}_{R}=\nabla w\quad{\mathcal{L}}^{d}\textup{-}\text{ a.e. in }A_{\rm out}^{i}.

Therefore,

(3.9)

\int_{A}f(x,\nabla v^{i}_{R})\,dx=\int_{A^{i}_{\rm in}}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+\int_{A^{i}_{\rm str}}f(x,\nabla v^{i}_{R})\,dx+\int_{A^{i}_{\rm out}}f(x,\nabla w)\,dx.

Exploiting (f2
′
), (f3
′
), and (
3.8
), and recalling that
\text{Lip}(\psi_{R}^{i})=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{A^{i}}f(x,\nabla v^{i}_{R})\,dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{i}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{i}}|\nabla u|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{i})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{i}}|\nabla w|\,dx+\frac{c_{3}k^{1/2}}{c_{1}}\int_{A^{i}}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{i}),

\displaystyle\int_{A^{i}_{\rm out}}f(x,\nabla w)\,dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{R}_{u,w}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{u,w}^{R}).

These inequalites and (
3.9
) lead to

(3.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{A}f(x,\nabla v^{i}_{R})\,dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m\Big(\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A_{u,w}^{R}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{u,w}^{R})\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{3}k^{1/2}}{c_{1}}\int_{A}f(x,\nabla u)\,dx+2c_{3}\int_{A}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A).

To estimate the term depending on
D^{c}u
in
E^{f,g}
, we use Proposition
2.5
(ii) to rewrite

(3.11)

\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{D^{c}v^{i}_{R}}{|D^{c}v^{i}_{R}|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}v^{i}_{R}|=\int_{A_{\rm in}^{i}}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|\\
+\int_{A^{i}}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})}{|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|}\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|d|D^{c}u|+\int_{A^{i}_{\rm out}}f^{\infty}(x,0)\,d|D^{c}u|\\
\leq\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|+\int_{A^{i}}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})}{|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|}\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|d|D^{c}u|,

where we have used that
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and that
f^{\infty}(x,0)=0.
Taking advantage once again of (
3.3
) and of
\text{Lip}(\psi_{R}^{i})=1
, we infer that

\displaystyle\int_{A^{i}}f^{\infty}\Big(x\frac{\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})}{|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|}\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|d|D^{c}u|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{i}}|\nabla\psi_{R}^{i}(\widetilde{u}-\widetilde{w})|d|D^{c}u|\leq\frac{c_{3}k^{1/2}}{c_{1}}\int_{A^{i}}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|.

Together with the previous inequalities this leads to

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{D^{c}v^{i}_{R}}{|D^{c}v^{i}_{R}|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}v^{i}_{R}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{d|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{3}k^{1/2}}{c_{1}}\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}u}{|D^{c}u|}\Big)\,d|D^{c}u|.

We now estimate the surface term in
E^{f,g}
. To this scope, we first remark that
J_{v^{i}_{R}}\subset A\setminus A_{\rm reg}
and that for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e
x\in J_{v^{i}_{R}}
we have
\nu_{v^{i}_{R}}(x)=\nu_{u}(x)
. Moreover, since
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e.
x\in A
the approximate limit
\widetilde{w}(x)
exists, so that for
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e. every
x\in J_{v^{i}_{R}}
we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(v^{i}_{R})^{+}(x)=\widetilde{w}(x)+\psi_{R}^{i}(u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(v^{i}_{R})^{-}(x)=\widetilde{w}(x)+\psi_{R}^{i}(u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)).

For
i\in\{1,...,m\}
we introduce the following partition of
\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}:=\{x\in J_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon\widetilde{w}(x)\text{ exists}\}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{1}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i-1}R,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i-1}R\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{2}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x))|,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\in[\sigma^{i-1}R,\sigma^{i}R]\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{3}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|>\sigma^{i}R,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|>\sigma^{i}R\}\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{4}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i-1}R,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|>\sigma^{i}R\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{5}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|>\sigma^{i}R,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i-1}R\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{6}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\in[\sigma^{i-1}R,\sigma^{i}R],\,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\notin[\sigma^{i-1}R,\sigma^{i}R]\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle S^{i}_{7}:=\{x\in\widetilde{J}_{v^{i}_{R}}\colon\,|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\notin[\sigma^{i-1}R,\sigma^{i}R],\,\,|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\in[\sigma^{i-1}R,\sigma^{i}R]\}.

Note that for
\ell\in\{2,6,7\}

\displaystyle\text{if }S^{i}_{\ell}\cap S^{j}_{\ell}\neq\mathchar 31\relax\text{\,\,\,\,then\,\, $|i-j|\leq 1$},

so that

(3.15)

\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\chi_{S^{i}_{2}}+\chi_{S^{i}_{6}}+\chi_{S^{i}_{7}})\leq 9.

By definition of
S^{i}_{1}
and of
S^{i}_{3}
, recalling (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), we have

(3.16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{S_{1}^{i}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}=\int_{S_{1}^{i}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

(3.17)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{S_{3}^{i}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}=\int_{S_{3}^{i}}g(x,0,\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}=0.

Since
\text{Lip}(\psi_{R}^{i})=1
, we have
|[\psi_{R}^{i}(u-w)]|\leq|[u-w]|=|[u]|
in
\widetilde{J}_{{v^{i}_{R}}}
. Hence, by (g2) and (g3) we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{S_{2}^{i}\cup S_{6}^{i}\cup S_{7}^{i}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{3}k\int_{S_{2}^{i}\cup S_{6}^{i}\cup S_{7}^{i}}|[\psi_{R}^{i}(u-w)]|\land 1\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}k\int_{S_{2}^{i}\cup S_{6}^{i}\cup S_{7}^{i}}|[u]|\land 1\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{3}k}{c_{1}}\int_{S_{2}^{i}\cup S_{6}^{i}\cup S_{7}^{i}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}.

We are left with estimating the surface integral over
S_{4}^{i}
and
S^{i}_{5}
. By definition, if
x\in S^{i}_{4}
then
|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|<\sigma^{i-1}R
and
|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|>\sigma^{i}R.

Therefore,

(3.19)

\displaystyle|[u](x)|=|u^{+}(x)-u^{-}(x)|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|u^{-}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|-|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}R-\sigma^{i-1}R=\sigma^{i-1}R(\sigma-1)\geq c_{6}k\sigma^{i-1}R,

where we have used that
\sigma>c_{6}k+1
. On the other hand,
[v^{i}_{R}](x)=u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)
so that

c_{6}k|[v^{i}_{R}]|=c_{6}k|u^{+}(x)-\widetilde{w}(x)|\leq c_{6}k\sigma^{i-1}R.

From these inequalities we deduce that
c_{6}k|[v^{i}_{R}]|\leq|[u]|
, which by (g6) implies

g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\leq g(x,[u],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})=g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\quad\text{ ${\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}$-a.e. in $S^{i}_{4}$}.

The same argument shows that
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}
-a.e. in
S^{i}_{5}
it holds

g(x,[v_{R}^{i}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\leq g(x,[u],\nu_{u}).

From these last two inequalities we get

(3.20)

\int_{S_{4}^{i}\cup S^{i}_{5}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\leq\int_{S_{4}^{i}\cup S^{i}_{5}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}.

Combining (
3.16
)-(
3.20
), we obtain

\int_{J_{v^{i}_{R}}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\leq\int_{J_{u}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}+\frac{c_{3}k}{c_{1}}\int_{S^{i}_{2}\cup S^{i}_{6}\cup S^{i}_{7}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1},

which, in light of (
3.15
), implies that

(3.21)

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{J_{v^{i}_{R}}}g(x,[v^{i}_{R}],\nu_{v^{i}_{R}})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\leq\Big(m+\frac{9c_{3}k}{c_{1}}\Big)\int_{J_{u}}g(x,[u],\nu_{u})\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}.

Finally, from (
3.10
), (
3.12
), and (
3.21
) we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E^{f,g}(v^{i}_{R},A)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{f,g}(u,A)+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{R}_{u,w}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{R}_{u,w})

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{m}\left(E^{f,g}(u,A)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)+\int_{A}|\nabla w|\,dx\right),

where
C:=\max\{9c_{3}k/c_{1},2c_{3}k^{1/2},c_{4}\}
. This shows that
E^{f,g}
satisfies (g).

To see that
E^{f,g}
satisfies (h) it is enough to verify it for
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and for
u|_{A}\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Let
\lambda\leq 1/c_{6}
and let
R\in SO(k)
. Note that by (
3.1
) we also have
\lambda\leq(c_{1}/c_{3})k^{-1/2}
. We set
v:=\lambda Ru
. By (f2’), (f3), and (g6) we can estimate

\displaystyle E^{f,g}(v,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A}f(x,\nabla v)\,dx+\int_{A}f^{\infty}\Big(x,\frac{dD^{c}v}{d|D^{c}v|},\Big)\,d|D^{c}v|+\int_{A\cap J_{u}}g(x,[v],\nu)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{3}k^{1/2}\lambda\Big(\int_{A}|\nabla u|\,dx+|D^{c}u|(A)\Big)+\int_{A}g(x,[u],\nu)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{f,g}(u,A)+(c_{4}+c_{2}){\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A),

concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

It follows from Remark
2.9
and Proposition
3.13

that the functional
V
of Definition
2.6
belongs to
\mathfrak{E}
.

The following lemma provides an estimate for smooth truncations of the form
\psi_{R}\circ u
, which can be considered as an extension to the vector-valued case of the simpler inequality (
3.6
), valid in the scalar case. The proof
heavily relies on property (g) of Definition
3.8
. More refined versions of the following result will be presented in the forthcoming sections.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, let
u_{1}\in L^{0}(A_{1};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
u_{2}\in L^{0}(A_{2};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and for
j=1,2
let
E(\cdot,A_{j})\colon L^{0}(A_{j};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\to[0,+\infty]
be a a lower semicontinuous functional satisfying
(c2
′
)
and
(g)
of Definition
3.8
with
B=A_{1}
and
B=A_{2}
. Then there exists a strictly increasing sequence
R_{m}>0
, with
R_{m}\to+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
, such that

(3.22)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}E(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u_{j},A_{j})=E(u_{j},A_{j})

for
j=1,2
.

Proof.

For every sequence
R_{m}\to+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
, we have that
\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u\to u_{j}
in
L^{0}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
as
m\to+\infty
, so that the lower semicontinuity of
E(\cdot,A_{j})
with respect to the topology of
L^{0}(A_{j};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
implies that

(3.23)

E(u_{j},A_{j})\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}E(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u_{j},A_{j})

for
j=1,2.

Assume now that
E(u_{1},A_{1})
and
E(u_{2},A_{2})
are both finite.
We can choose a sequence
r_{m}
converging to
+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
such that

c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{u_{j},0}^{r_{m}})\leq\frac{1}{m}\quad\text{ for every }m\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and for $j=1,2$}.

From (g) of Definition
3.8
applied with
w=0
, we obtain that there exists
i(m)\in\{1,...,m\}
, such that

\displaystyle E(\psi_{r_{m}}^{i(m)}\circ u_{1},A_{1})+E(\psi_{r_{m}}^{i(m)}\circ u_{2},A_{2})

\displaystyle\leq E(u_{1},A_{1})+E(u_{2},A_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2+C(E(u_{1},A_{1})+E(u_{2},A_{2}))+2c_{4}C{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A))}{m}.

We now set
R_{m}:=\sigma^{i(m)}r_{m}
, so that
\psi^{i(m)}_{r_{m}}=\psi_{R_{m}}
. Hence, the previous inequality gives

\limsup_{m\to\infty}(E(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u_{1},A_{1})+E(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u_{2},A_{2}))\leq E(u_{1},A_{1})+E(u_{2},A_{2}).

Combining this last inequality with (
3.23
), we obtain (
3.22
) in the case where
E(u_{1},A_{1})
and
E(u_{2},A_{2})
are both finite. A simpler proof yields the result when in the other cases.
∎

4.
A compactness result

The main result of this section is a compactness theorem for the class
\mathfrak{E}
. The strategy here adopted is the one of
[
21
, Lemma 3.24]
. As already mentioned, the main difference in the proof this compactness result in the vectorial case lies in the different type of truncation used, which is dealt with in Lemma
4.5
below.

In what follows, given a sequence
(E_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{E}
and
A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, we set

(4.1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{\prime}(\cdot,A):=\Gamma\textup{-}\liminf_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(\cdot,A)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,E^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,A):=\Gamma\textup{-}\limsup_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(\cdot,A),

(4.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{-}(\cdot,A):=\sup_{A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}(A)}E^{\prime}(\cdot,A)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,E^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\cdot,A):=\sup_{A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}(A)}E^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,A^{\prime}),

where the
\Gamma\textup{-}\liminf
and the
\Gamma\textup{-}\limsup
are computed with respect to the topology of
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Given
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, it is immediate to check that
E^{\prime}(u,\cdot)
,
E^{\prime\prime}(u,\cdot)
,
E^{\prime}_{-}(u,\cdot),
and
E_{-}^{\prime\prime}(u,\cdot)
are all increasing set functions.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{E}
be a sequence of functionals. Then there exists a subsequence of
(E_{n})_{n}
, not relabelled, and a functional
E\in\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
such that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
the sequence
E_{n}(\cdot,A)

\Gamma
-converges to
E(\cdot,A)
in the topology of
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
.

Proof.

By a compactness theorem for
\Gamma
-convergence of increasing functionals (see
[
17
, Theorem 16.9]
), there exists a subsequence of
(E_{n})_{n}
, not relabelled, such that

(4.3)

E(u,A):=E^{\prime}_{-}(u,A)=E^{\prime\prime}_{-}(u,A)\quad\text{for every $u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})$ and }\text{for }A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}).\\

Thus, we can define
E\colon L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\to[0+\infty]
as

(4.4)

E(u,B)=\inf\{E(u,A):\,\,A\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}),\,\,B\subset A\}\quad\text{for every }B\in\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d}).

We claim that
E\in\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
and that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have
E(\cdot,A)=E^{\prime}(\cdot,A)=E^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,A)
.

From some general
\Gamma
-convergence results (see
[
17
, Proposition 16.15]
), it follows that
E
satisfies property (a) of Definition
3.8
.

To show that
E
enjoys also property (b), we make use of the De Giorgi-Letta Criterion for measures
[
22
]
(see also
[
17
, Theorem 14.23]
for the particular version of the theorem here employed). As already mentioned, for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
the set function
E(u,\cdot)
is increasing. The inner regularity follows by definition of
E(u,\cdot)
, while superadditivity is a consequence of
[
17
, Proposition 16.12]
and of the fact that
E_{n}
satisfies (b) of Definition
3.8
. We are left with proving that
E(u,\cdot)
is subadditive. To prove this, we make use of a truncation argument and of the following estimate for which we refer to
[
21
, Lemma 3.19]
, the proof in the vectorial case being the same as in the scalar case.

Lemma 4.2

(
[
21
, Lemma 3.19]
)
.

Let
(E_{n})\subset\mathfrak{E}
be a sequence of functionals and let
A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime},A,U\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, with
A^{\prime}\subset\subset A^{\prime\prime}\subset\subset A
. Let
u\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and let
(w_{n})_{n},(v_{n})_{n}\subset L^{1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
be two sequence converging to
u
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and such that

(w_{n}|_{A})\subset BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
(v_{n}|_{U})\subset BV(U;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then for every
\delta>0
there exists a sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d},[0,1])
, with
\text{supp}(\varphi_{n})\subset A^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi_{n}=1
in a neighborhood of
\overline{A}^{\prime}
, such that the functions
u_{n}
defined by

u_{n}:=\varphi_{n}w_{n}+(1-\varphi_{n})v_{n}

converge to
u
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
u_{n}|_{A^{\prime}\cup U}\in BV(A^{\prime}\cup U;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to+\infty}E_{n}(u_{n},A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq(1+\delta)\liminf_{n\to+\infty}(E_{n}(w_{n},A)+E_{n}(v_{n},U))+\delta,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to+\infty}E_{n}(u_{n},A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq(1+\delta)\limsup_{n\to+\infty}(E_{n}(w_{n},A)+E_{n}(v_{n},U))+\delta.

We now consider the truncated functions
\psi_{R}\circ u
and prove that
E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,\cdot)
and
E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,\cdot)
satisfy a weak subadditivity inequality.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}\subset\mathfrak{E}
be a sequence of functionals, let
E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime\prime}
be the functionals defined by (
4.1
), let
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and let
A^{\prime},A,U\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, with
A^{\prime}\subset\subset A
. Then for every
R>0
,
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U).

Proof.

We prove (
4.5
).
Without loss of generality, we assume that
E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)
and
E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U)
are both finite.
Let
w_{n},v_{n}\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
be two sequence of functions converging in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
to
\psi_{R}\circ u
and such that

(4.7)

\limsup_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(w_{n},A)=E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\liminf_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(v_{n},U)=E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U).

We fix a subsequence
(E_{n_{h}})_{h}
of
(E_{n})_{n}
such that

\lim_{h\to+\infty}E_{n_{h}}(v_{n_{h}},U)=\liminf_{n\to\infty}E_{n}(v_{n},U)=E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U).

Without loss of generality we may assume that there exists
M>0
such that

E_{n_{h}}(w_{n_{h}},A)<M\quad\text{ and }\quad E_{n_{h}}(v_{n_{h}},U)<M,

for every
h\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Remark
3.12
then implies
(w_{n_{h}}|_{A})_{h}\subset GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
,
(v_{n_{h}}|_{U})_{h}\subset GBV_{\star}(U;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
.
By property (g) of Definition
3.8
for every
h,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we can find
i_{h,m},j_{h,m}\in\{1,...,m\}
such that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684ptE_{n_{h}}(\psi^{i_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ w_{n_{h}},A)\leq E_{n_{h}}(w_{n_{h}},A)+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{\sigma R}_{w_{n_{h}},0})+C\frac{M+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)}{m},

(4.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684ptE_{n_{h}}(\psi^{j_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ v_{n_{h}},U)\leq E_{n_{h}}(v_{n_{h}},U)+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(U^{\sigma R}_{v_{n_{h}},0})+C\frac{M+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(U)}{m}.

Since
w_{n_{h}}
and
v_{n_{h}}
converge in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
to
\psi_{R}\circ u
as
n\to+\infty
,
\|\psi_{\sigma R}^{i_{h,m}}\|_{L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})}\leq\sigma^{m+1}R
,
\|\psi_{\sigma R}^{j_{h,m}}\|_{L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})}\leq\sigma^{m+1}R
, and
\|\psi_{R}\circ u\|_{L^{\infty}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})}\leq\sigma R
, for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we infer that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\psi_{\sigma R}^{i_{h,m}}\circ w_{n_{h}}\to\psi_{R}\circ u\,\,\,\text{ in }L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\,\text{ as }h\to+\infty,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\psi_{\sigma R}^{j_{h,m}}\circ v_{n_{h}}\to\psi_{R}\circ u\,\,\,\text{ in }L^{1}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\,\text{ as }h\to+\infty,

(4.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{h\to\infty}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{\sigma R}_{w_{n_{h}},0})={\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{\psi_{R}\circ u,0}^{\sigma R})=0,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{h\to\infty}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(U^{\sigma R}_{v_{n_{h}},0})={\mathcal{L}}^{d}(U_{\psi_{R}\circ u,0}^{\sigma R})=0.

For every
V\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we set

(4.12)

\widehat{E}^{\prime}(\cdot,V):=\Gamma\textup{-}\liminf_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\cdot,V)\,\,\text{ and }\,\,\widehat{E}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,V):=\Gamma\textup{-}\limsup_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\cdot,V),\\

and observe that

(4.13)

E^{\prime}(\cdot,V)\leq\widehat{E}^{\prime}(\cdot,V)\quad\text{and }\quad\widehat{E}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,V)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,V).

By (
4.7
)-(
4.11
), we deduce that

(4.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\limsup_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\psi^{i_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ w_{n_{h}},A)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+C\frac{M+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)}{m}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\liminf_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\psi^{j_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ v_{n_{h}},U)\leq E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U)+C\frac{M+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(U)}{m}.

By Proposition
2.3
we have that
\psi_{\sigma R}^{i_{h,m}}\circ w_{n_{h}}|_{A}\in BV(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and that
\psi_{\sigma R}^{j_{h,m}}\circ v_{n_{h}}|{U}\in BV(U,{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Thus, for
\delta>0
and for every
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we may apply Lemma
4.2
to obtain a sequence of functions
(u^{m}_{h})_{h}\subset BV(A^{\prime}\cup U;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
such that
u^{m}_{h}\to\psi_{R}\circ u
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
as
h\to+\infty
and such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\liminf_{h\to+\infty}E_{n_{h}}(u^{m}_{h},A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq(1+\delta)\liminf_{h\to\infty}\Big(E_{n_{h}}(\psi^{i_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ w_{n_{h}},A)+E_{n_{h}}(\psi_{\sigma_{R}}^{j_{h,m}}\circ v_{n_{h}},U)\Big)+\delta

\displaystyle\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\limsup_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\psi^{i_{h,m}}_{\sigma R}\circ w_{n_{h}},A)+(1+\delta)\liminf_{h\to+\infty}E_{n_{h}}(\psi_{\sigma_{R}}^{j_{h,m}}\circ v_{n_{h}},U)+\delta

This inequality, combined with (
4.14
), and (
4.15
), yields

\liminf_{h\to\infty}E_{n_{h}}(u^{m}_{h},A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq(1+\delta)\Big(E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U)+\frac{S}{m}\Big)+\delta,

where
S
is a positive constant independent of
m
. Recalling that
u^{m}_{h}\to\psi_{R}\circ u
as
h\to+\infty
, by (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) we obtain that

E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq\widehat{E}^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq(1+\delta)\Big(E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,U)+\frac{S}{m}\Big)+\delta.

Letting
m\to+\infty
and
\delta\to 0^{+}
, we obtain (
4.5
).

A similar, but easier, argument shows that (
4.6
) holds true.
∎

To prove a weak subadditivity inequality for
E^{\prime}(u,\cdot)
we will approximate
E^{\prime}(u,A)
by
E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)
. For technical reasons, this approximation result is obtained using property
(g)
of Definition
3.8
for
E^{\prime}
, which is proved in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathfrak{E}
, let
E^{\prime}
be the functional defined by (
4.1
), let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and let
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then for every
R>0
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

(4.16)

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E^{\prime}(w+\psi^{i}_{R}\circ(u-w),A)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A)+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A^{R}_{u,w}}|\nabla w|+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{R}_{u,w})\\
+\frac{C}{m}\left(E^{\prime}(u,A)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)+\int_{A}|\nabla w|\,dx\right),

where
C=\{9c_{3}k/c_{1},2c_{3}k^{1/2},c_{4}\}
.

Proof.

Let us fix
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
w\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}).
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
E^{\prime}(u,A)<+\infty
. Let
(u_{n})_{n}\subset L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
be a sequence of functions converging to
u
in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and such that

(4.17)

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}E_{n}(u_{n},A)=E^{\prime}(u,A).

Fix
R>0
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For every
i\in\{1,...,m\}
, we set
v^{i}_{n}:=w+\psi_{R}^{i}\circ(u_{n}-w)
and
v^{i}:=w+\psi_{R}\circ(u-w)
. Note that for every
i\in\{1,...,m\}
the sequence
(v^{i}_{n})_{n}
converges to
v^{i}
in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, so that by Definition of
E^{\prime}
we have

(4.18)

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E^{\prime}(v^{i},A)\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\liminf_{n\to+\infty}E_{n}(v^{i}_{n},A)\leq\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E_{n}(v^{i}_{n},A).

By property (g) and by (
4.17
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E_{n}(v^{i}_{n},A)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E_{n}(u_{n},A)+\frac{C}{m}\Big(\displaystyle E_{n}(u_{n},A)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)+\int_{A}|\nabla w|\Big)

\displaystyle+c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A_{u_{n},w}^{R}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{u_{n},w}^{R}).

By (
4.17
), this inequality gives

(4.19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}E^{\prime}(v^{i},A)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(u,A)+\frac{C}{m}\Big(\displaystyle E^{\prime}(u,A)+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)+\int_{A}|\nabla w|\Big)

\displaystyle+\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\Big(c_{3}k^{1/2}\int_{A_{u_{n},w}^{R}}|\nabla w|\,dx+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A_{u_{n},w}^{R})\Big).

Since
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, we have that

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\chi_{A^{R}_{u_{n},w}(x)}\leq\chi_{A^{R}_{u,w}}(x)\quad\text{for ${\mathcal{L}}^{d}\textup{-}$a.e. }x\in A.

Thus, from Fatou’s Lemma, we deduce that

(4.20)

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\Big(\int_{A_{u_{n},w}^{R}}|\nabla w|\,dx+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{R}_{u_{n},w})\Big)\leq\int_{A^{R}_{u,w}}|\nabla w|\,dx+{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A^{R}_{w}),

which combined with (
4.19
) yields (
4.16
).
∎

To obtain the weak subadditivity of
E^{\prime}(u,\cdot)
we show that
E^{\prime}(u,A)
can be approximated by
E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)
for a suitable choice of
R
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathfrak{E}
, let
E^{\prime}
be the functional defined by (
4.1
), let
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
,
u_{1}\in L^{0}(A_{1};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
u_{2}\in L^{0}(A_{2};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Then there exists an increasing sequence
R_{m}>0
, with
R_{m}\to+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
, such that

(4.21)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u_{j},A_{j})=E^{\prime}(u_{j},A_{j})

for
j=1,2.

Proof.

It is immediate to check that
E^{\prime}
satisfies property (c2), and by Lemma
4.4
, we have that
E^{\prime}
satisfies property (g) for
B=A_{1}
and
B=A_{2}
. Since the functional
E^{\prime}(\cdot,A_{j})
is lower semicontinuous with respect to the topology of
L^{0}(A_{j};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
for
j=1,2
(see
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
), an application of Lemma
3.15
then proves the claim.
∎

We now prove a weak subadditivity inequality for
E^{\prime}(u,\cdot)
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of functionals in
\mathfrak{E}
and let
E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}
be defined by (
4.1
). Assume that there exists a functional
E\colon L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\to[0,+\infty]
such that (
4.3
) holds. Let
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, and
A^{\prime},A,U\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
with
A^{\prime}\subset\!\subset A
. Then we have

(4.22)

E^{\prime}(u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A)+E^{\prime}(u,U).

Proof.

By
[
17
, Proposition 15.15]
we may choose
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
, with
A^{\prime}\subset\subset A^{\prime\prime}\subset\subset A
, such that

(4.23)

E^{\prime\prime}(v,A^{\prime\prime})=E^{\prime}(v,A^{\prime\prime})=E(v,A^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{for every }v\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}).

Lemma
4.5
implies that there exists
R_{m}\to+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
such that

(4.24)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{m\to+\infty}E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,A^{\prime\prime})=E^{\prime}(u,A^{\prime\prime}),

(4.25)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{m\to+\infty}E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,U)=E^{\prime}(u,U).

Thanks, to Lemma
4.3
, we
obtain that

E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,A^{\prime\prime})+E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,U)\leq E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,A)+E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,U),

where in the equality we have used (
4.23
). Since
\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u\to u
in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
as
m\to+\infty
, by the lower semicontinuity of
E^{\prime}
, (
4.24
), and (
4.25
) we obtain

E^{\prime}(u,A^{\prime}\cup U)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A^{\prime\prime})+E^{\prime}(u,U)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A)+E^{\prime}(u,U),

concluding the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove the subadditivity of
E(u,\cdot)
on
\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
E_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathfrak{E}
for which (
4.3
) holds for some functional
E
. Then for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A,U\in\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

E(u,A\cup U)\leq E(u,A)+E(u,U).

Proof.

The result follows from Lemma
4.6
and by standard arguments (see for instance the proof of
[
17
, Lemma 18.4]
).
∎

Finally, we show that
(E_{n}(\cdot,A))_{n}

\Gamma
-converges for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(E_{n})_{n}
be a sequence of functionals in
\mathfrak{E}
, let
E^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime}
be the functionals defined by (
4.1
). Assume that there exists a functional
E
such that (
4.3
) holds. Then
E(u,A)=E^{\prime}(u,A)=E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)
for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.

Proof.

Fix
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. We first show that
E(u,A)=E^{\prime}(u,A)
. Since
E(u,A)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A)
, it is enough to show the converse inequality. Without loss of generality we may suppose that
E(u,A)<+\infty
. It is immediate to check that
E^{\prime}
and
E
satisfies properties (c2) Definition
3.8
on
\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and using Remark
2.9
we check that
E^{\prime}
and
E
also satisfy (c1) on
\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. Hence,
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider a compact set
K\subset A
such that

(4.26)

c_{3}V(u,A\setminus K)+c_{4}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A\setminus K)\leq\varepsilon.

Consider now
A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{c}(A)
with
K\subset A^{\prime}\subset\subset A^{\prime\prime}
and let
U=A\setminus K
. Note that
A^{\prime}\cup U=A
. Hence, by Lemma
4.6
to obtain

(4.27)

E^{\prime}(u,A)\leq E^{\prime}(u,A^{\prime\prime})+E^{\prime}(u,A\setminus K)\leq E(u,A)+\varepsilon,

where in the last inequality we have used (
4.3
), property (c2) for
E^{\prime}
and (
4.26
). These arguments shows that
E(u,A)=E^{\prime}(u,A)
for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.

We now prove that
E(u,A)=E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)
. Since
E(u,A)\leq E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)
it is enough to show that
E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)\leq E(u,A)
. It is not restrictive to assume that
E(u,A)<+\infty
, so that
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Given
R>0
, we can exploit the same argument used to obtain (
4.27
), replacing (
4.22
) of Lemma
4.6
with (
4.6
), to show that for every
\varepsilon>0

(4.28)

E^{\prime\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)\leq E(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+\varepsilon=E^{\prime}(\psi_{R}\circ u,A)+\varepsilon,

where we have used the equality
E=E^{\prime}
. By Lemma
4.5
there exists a positive sequence
R_{m}\to+\infty
such that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}E^{\prime}(\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u,A)=E^{\prime}(u,A)=E(u,A).

Since
\psi_{R_{m}}\circ u\to u
in
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
as
m\to+\infty
, exploiting the lower semicontinuity of
E^{\prime\prime}
and this last equality, we deduce from (
4.28
) that

E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)\leq E(u,A)+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we get
E^{\prime\prime}(u,A)\leq E(u,A)
, concluding the proof.
∎

Continuation of the Proof of Theorem
4.1
.
Thanks to Lemma
4.8
, for every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have that

(4.29)

E(u,A)=E^{\prime}(u,A)=E^{\prime\prime}(u,A).

We are left with proving that
E\in\mathfrak{E}_{\it sc}
. We already noted that
E
satisfies (a) of Definition
3.8
. From Lemma
4.7
and De Giorgi-Letta theorem, we deduce that
E
satisfies (b) as well.
Properties (c2)-(f) can be derived arguing as in
[
21
, Theorem 3.16]
.

By Remark
2.9
and (
4.29
), we infer that for ever

u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
we have

c_{1}V(u,A)-c_{2}{\mathcal{L}}^{d}(A)\leq E(u,A).

By inner regularity the inequality can be extended to
\mathcal{A}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and, recalling (
4.4
), to
\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
.
Property (g) for
E
on
\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
is proved in Lemma
4.4
, while (h) is trivial. The extension of (g) and (h) to all Borel sets can easily be obtained using (
4.4
).

Finally, by
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
the functionals
E^{\prime}(\cdot,A)
and
E^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,A)
are lower semicontinuous with respect to the convergence of
L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
. Since
E=E^{\prime}=E^{\prime\prime}
, we conclude that
E\in\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
.
∎

5.
Partial Integral representation

In this Section we present and prove a (partial) representation result for functionals in
\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
. We postpone the full representation to Section
7
, where we will work with functionals in
\mathfrak{E}_{\rm sc}
satisfying an additional property.

We introduce a splitting of
E
that mimics the structure of functionals in
E^{f,g}
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
E\colon L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})\times\mathcal{B}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})\to[0,+\infty]
be a functional satisfying (b) and (c2) of Definition
3.8
, and let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
. For every
u\in L^{0}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d};{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, we introduce
E^{a}(u,\cdot)
,
E^{s}(u,\cdot)
,
E^{c}(u,\cdot)
, and
E^{j}(u,A)
as the Borel measures on
\mathcal{B}(A)
defined as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{a}(u,\cdot)\,\,\text{is the absolutely continuous part of $E(u,\cdot)$ with respect to }{\mathcal{L}}^{d},

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{s}(u,\cdot)\,\,\text{is the singular part of $E(u,\cdot)$ with respect to }{\mathcal{L}}^{d},

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{j}(u,B)=E^{s}(u,B\cap J_{u})\,\,\text{ for every }B\in\mathcal{B}(A),

\displaystyle\displaystyle E^{c}(u,B)=E^{s}(u,B\setminus J_{u})\,\,\text{ for every }B\in\mathcal{B}(A).

Note that by arguing as in
[
21
, Remark 4.2]
, we see that when
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
, the measures
E^{j}(u,\cdot)
and
E^{c}(u,\cdot)
are the absolutely continuous parts with respect to
{\mathcal{H}}^{d-1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}J_{u}
and
|D^{c}u|
, respectively. Additionally, for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
it holds

E(u,\cdot)=E^{a}(u,\cdot)+E^{c}(u,\cdot)+E^{j}(u,\cdot)\,\,\,\text{ on }\mathcal{B}(A).

The aim of this section is to show that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}_{c}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
and for every
u\in GBV_{\star}(A;{{\mathbb{R}}}^{k})
we may represent the two measures
E^{a}(u,\cdot)
and
E^{j}(u,\cdot)
as integrals.

As in
[
21
]
, the idea is to take advantage of the representation results of
[
9
]
for functionals on
